Bounded Exponential Sums with Multiplicative Coefficients
Ihor Pylaiev, Fred Tyrrell

Abstract
. We investigate when the exponential sum ∑ n ≤ x f ⁡ ( n ) ​ e 2 ​ UNKNOWN ​ i ​ n ​ UNKNOWN \mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29029^{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28953\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29038\mathchar 28939} is bounded, for a multiplicative function f \mathchar 29030 and α ∈ ℝ \mathchar 28939\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010} . We show that under certain natural assumptions, this exponential sum is bounded only when f \mathchar 29030 closely resembles a periodic function. For completely multiplicative functions or those taking only finitely many values, we obtain sharper classification results, including a full classification (up to a measure-zero set of α \mathchar 28939 ) in the completely multiplicative case.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background and motivation

Exponential sums involving multiplicative functions

form a central object of study in analytic number theory, bridging additive and multiplicative structures in the integers. For a multiplicative function
\mathchar 29030\mathchar 12346\mathbb{\mathchar 29006}\mathchar 12833\mathbb{\mathchar 28995}
, we consider sums of the form

\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29030}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\mathchar 24635\mathchar 28939\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29029\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\mathchar 28939\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

\mathchar 29029\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28939\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29029^{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28953\mathchar 29033\mathchar 28939}
and
\mathchar 28939\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}
is fixed. Understanding the size and behaviour of exponential sums with multiplicative coefficients sheds light on how multiplicative functions interact with additive characters, with applications to questions of equidistribution modulo 1, the pseudorandomness of arithmetic functions, and the classification of multiplicative functions according to their additive correlations.

A classical result of Daboussi
[
Dab75
]
shows that if
\mathchar 29030
is bounded and satisfies certain orthogonality conditions, then
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29030}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\mathchar 24635\mathchar 28939\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29039\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785
for all irrational
\mathchar 28939
. Montgomery and Vaughan
[
MV77
]
further developed general bounds for such sums, achieving a close to optimal upper bound for these sums on major arcs. Several generalisations and extensions of these results have since appeared, and exponential sums involving multiplicative functions remains an active area of research within analytic number theory; see, for example, the work of Daboussi-Delange
[
DD82
]
, Kátai
[
Kát86
]
, Bachmann
[
Bac99
]
, and Matomäki-Radziwiłł-Tao-Teräväinen-Ziegler
[
MRTTZ23
]
.

More recently, Granville and Soundararajan
[
GS14
]
formalised the orthogonality between additive characters and multiplicative functions via the
pretentious approach
, introducing a quantitative framework to measure the extent to which a multiplicative function mimics a structured object such as
\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29044}\mathchar 28959\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785
. This perspective unifies and generalises many earlier results by showing that cancellation in exponential sums is intimately tied to the ‘distance’ between
\mathchar 29030
and such structured functions, and was recently used by de la Bretèche and Granville
[
BG22
]
to continue the study of these exponential sums over minor arcs.

This paper investigates the most extreme version of this cancellation phenomenon, namely when
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29030}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28939\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29048\right\delimiter 84054785
is bounded. This is motivated by work on
discrepancy
problems, in particular Tao’s solution to the Erdős Discrepancy Problem
[
Tao16
]
.

The Erdős Discrepancy Problem was a long-standing conjecture of Erdős
[
Erd57
]
, which states that for
any

\mathchar 29030\mathchar 12346\mathbb{\mathchar 29006}\mathchar 12833\{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\}
, the discrepancy

\sup_{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29038}\left\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29034\delimiter 84054785\right\delimiter 69640972

is unbounded. One of the key ingredients of Tao’s solution was the breakthrough of Matomäki-Radziwiłł
[
MR16
]
and a result from the first Polymath project, which showed that solving the problem in the case where
\mathchar 29030
is completely multiplicative was sufficient to prove the full conjecture. Numerous investigations into the discrepancy of various arithmetic functions have since appeared in the literature, including
[
Klu17
]
,
[
KM18
]
,
[
KMPT21
]
,
[

]

[

]

[

]
and
[

]

[

]

Particularly relevant to the work in this paper is the work of Frantzikinakis
[
Fra19
]
on the

Erdős discrepancy problem, which extends Tao’s ideas by asking for which weight functions

it is possible to find sequences
\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\}
such that the weighted discrepancy

\sup_{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29038}\left\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29034\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29047\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29034\delimiter 84054785\right\delimiter 69640972

\left\delimiter 0{\mathchar 4944\displaylimits\limits_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 29029\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\mathchar 28939\right\delimiter 84054785}\right\delimiter 0\left\delimiter 0{\mathchar 29029\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28939\right\delimiter 84054785{{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29029\left\delimiter 67273472\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 29048\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 28939\right\delimiter 84054785\over\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29029\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28939\right\delimiter 84054785}}}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 12820{{\mathchar 28722\over\left\delimiter 0{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29029\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28939\right\delimiter 84054785}\right\delimiter 0}}\mathchar 24891

Remark 1.5
.

For discussion of the optimality of our results, in particular ?THM?

\{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 12346\mathchar 29030\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29040\right\delimiter 84054785\mathrel{{{{\mathchar 566\mathchar 61}}}}\mathchar 28959\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29040\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29044}\}

\mathchar 29044\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}

Remark 1.6
.

We note that the condition

\mathchar 4944\displaylimits\limits_{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 12346\mathchar 29030\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 29035}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\text{ for some }\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28721}{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 29040}}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 561

in ?THM?s
and
is equivalent to the condition

\mathchar 4944\displaylimits\limits_{\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12346\mathchar 29030\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 29035}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720}{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 29035}}}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 561\mathchar 24891

since
\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29040}\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28722}{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 29035}}}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 561
, and simply ensures that a positive proportion of the values of

1.3.
Overview of the paper

•

In Section
2
, we show that
\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29029\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\mathchar 28939\delimiter 84054785
being bounded implies that
\mathchar 29030
is of a relatively special form, namely that
\mathchar 29030
is ‘pretentious’. Roughly speaking, this means that
\mathchar 29030
‘pretends to be’ a Dirichlet character, i.e, a periodic function. Tao’s logarithmically averaged Elliott result from
[
Tao16a
]
will play a crucial role in this section, in addition to the language of ‘pretentious number theory’ of Granville-Soundararajan.

•

In Section
3
, we prove ?THM?s
and
, by combining the reduction from the previous section with sieve-theoretic, combinatorial, and analytic ideas based on the ‘rotation trick’ introduced by Klurman, Mangerel, Pohoata and Teräväinen in
[
KMPT21
]
to study similar questions about partial sums of completely multiplicative functions. In particular, we develop a version of the rotation trick which allows us to extend their methods to handle general multiplicative functions, rather than just completely multiplicative functions.

•

Section
4
addresses the remaining class of functions, providing a partial converse to ?THM?
and proving ?THM?
. This section is rather technical, and involves a detailed analysis of the relevant quantities, combining inductive, probabilistic, analytic and combinatorial arguments with various direct computations, as well as a reduction to Gauss sums.

•

In Section
5
, we discuss the sharpness of our main results and whether it would be possible to strengthen them. In particular, we construct examples showing that a statement of the strength of ?THM?s
and
does not hold in the general case, so we cannot conclude that the set of ‘exceptional’ primes is finite in ?THM?
.

2.
Reduction to pretentious functions

In this section, we show that being close to periodic is, in some sense, the only way
\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29029\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\mathchar 28939\delimiter 84054785
can be bounded. We will show that, if
\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29029\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\mathchar 28939\delimiter 84054785
is bounded, then
\mathchar 29030
must ‘look like’ a Dirichlet character. We will use the language of
pretentious functions
, as developed by Granville-Soundararajan
[
GS14
]
, to make precise what we mean later in this section.

2.1.
Dirichlet characters

We begin this section by showing that the exponential partial sums of a Dirichlet character are bounded, unless
\mathchar 28939
is a rational number whose denominator is a divisor of the period of the Dirichlet character.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\mathchar 28959
be a Dirichlet character of modulus
\mathchar 29037
, and let
\mathchar 28939\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}
be such that
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 28939\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\delimiter 1299214\hfil$\crcr$\textstyle\mathchar 562$\crcr}}}\mathbb{\mathchar 29018}
. Then

\mathchar 4944\displaylimits\limits_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 28959\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29029\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\mathchar 28939\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29007_{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28939}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

\mathchar 12349\left\delimiter 0{\mathchar 4944\displaylimits\limits_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720}^{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 28959\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29029\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042\mathchar 28939\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 4944\displaylimits\limits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12820\left\delimiter 0{{\mathchar 29048\over\mathchar 29037}}\right\delimiter 0}\mathchar 29029\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\mathchar 29037\mathchar 28939\right\delimiter 84054785}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 29037\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721

The

2.2.

Definition 2.2
.

Remark 2.3
.

Perhaps the most important property of the pretentious distance is that it satisfies the triangle inequality, that is,
\mathbb{\mathchar 28996}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29030\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24635\mathchar 29048\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235\mathbb{\mathchar 28996}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29032\mathchar 24635\mathchar 29048\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12821\mathbb{\mathchar 28996}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29030\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29032\mathchar 24635\mathchar 29048\right\delimiter 84054785
.

We will use Tao’s two-point correlation result from
[

]
, which shows that a multiplicative function with high self-correlation must resemble
\mathchar 28959\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29044}
for some Dirichlet character
\mathchar 28959
and
\mathchar 29044\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}

2.3.

to our problem. The following lemma uses a ‘van der Corput’ argument to show that any function with bounded partial sums, which is bounded away from zero except on possibly a small number of values, must exhibit some logarithmic correlation.

\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785
, which we have proved above. So suppose our formula is true for

and applying the induction assumption, we obtain

Lemma 4.7
.

[Special case of the Borel Normal number theorem]
For each natural number
\mathchar 29038

Proof of ?THM?
.

\mathchar 29030
is a Dirichlet character of modulus
\mathchar 29037
. We know by ?THM?
that

for

\mathchar 29037\mathchar 28939\mathrel{{\mathchar 566\mathchar 562}}\mathbb{\mathchar 29018}
. Note that this is a countable set, and so it has measure zero. This proves the

Suppose now that
\mathchar 29030
is a completely multiplicative function and

\{\mathchar 28939\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29012}\mathchar 12346\limsup_{\mathchar 29048\mathchar 12833\mathchar 561}\left\delimiter 0{\mathchar 4944\displaylimits\limits_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29029\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28939\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 12604\mathchar 561\}

and real number

such that

\{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 12346\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29040\delimiter 84054785\mathrel{{{{\mathchar 566\mathchar 61}}}}\mathchar 28959\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29040\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29040^{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29044}\}\text{ is finite}\mathchar 314

5.
Sharpness of main results

It is tempting to wonder whether our main results can be strengthened, i.e. whether a result like ?THM?
or ?THM?
can be obtained in the general case. The following two propositions provide counterexamples, and show that for general multiplicative functions on the unit circle, ?THM?
is in fact close to optimal in some sense.

.

Proposition 5.2
.
